## Page 1


VERTEX SUPERALGEBRAS FOR HYPERTORIC VARIETIES
AND 3D ABELIAN GAUGE THEORIES
TOMOYUKI ARAKAWA,a,b ANDREA E. V. FERRARIc,d,e AND SVEN MÖLLERf,g
Abstract. Hypertoric (or toric hyperkähler) varieties are a class of symplectic
singularities and their resolutions, obtained as Hamiltonian reductions of a
symplectic vector space acted on by a torus. In physics, they appear as Higgs
(and Coulomb) branches of 3d N = 4 supersymmetric quantum field theories
with abelian gauge group.
In this work, we construct an ℏ-adic (in the sense of microlocalisation) sheaf
of vertex operator superalgebras over a given smooth hypertoric variety. Its
global sections give the A-twisted boundary vertex operator superalgebra of the
corresponding 3d gauge theory. We use this to prove that the associated affine
variety of this hypertoric vertex operator superalgebra recovers the singular
hypertoric variety.
This proves the 3d Higgs branch conjecture for a large
class of boundary vertex operator superalgebras. In particular, these vertex
operator superalgebras are quasi-lisse.
This is in contrast to the (purely even) hypertoric vertex operator algebras
(and their ℏ-adic localisations) constructed previously by Kuwabara as global
sections of sheaves on families of universal Poisson deformations of the hyper-
toric varieties. These are generally not quasi-lisse. We show that the vertex
operator superalgebras defined in this paper are (fermionic) simple-current ex-
tensions of those defined by Kuwabara, and investigate the consequences for
symplectic duality and characters. We observe that the latter are upgraded
from partial (or false) theta functions to quasimodular forms.

1. Introduction
Toric hyperkähler varieties, or hypertoric varieties for short, are hyperkähler (or
quaternionic) analogues of toric varieties. They were introduced (as manifolds) by
Bielawski and Dancer [BD00] by applying the hyperkähler quotient construction of
[HKLR87] to a torus G = (C×)M acting on a quaternionic vector space T ∗V = C2N.
Here, we treat them as algebraic varieties (equipped with the Zariski topology), as
described in [HS02, PW07, Pro08],
Yδ(∆) = µ−1(0)//δG = Proj
 M
m∈Z≥0
C[µ−1(0)]G,mδ
obtained as algebraic symplectic or GIT quotients associated with the algebraic
action of G on the symplectic vector space T ∗V , encoded in the integer weight
matrix ∆: ZN →ZM. Here, δ: G →C× is a choice of effective stability parameter.
The hypertoric variety Yδ(∆) is projective over the affine quotient
Y0(∆) := µ−1(0)//G = Spec C[µ−1(0)]G.
The latter is a symplectic singularity in the sense of [Bea00]. If ∆is unimodular and
δ generic, Yδ(∆) is smooth and the natural projective morphism π: Yδ(∆) →Y0(∆)
is a conical symplectic resolution. We assume this from now on. In particular,
Y0(∆) is normal and π is birational, which implies that OYδ(∆)(Yδ(∆)) = C[Y0(∆)],
i.e. Yδ(∆) and Y0(∆) have the same global functions.
Analogously to the celebrated Beilinson-Bernstein quantisation-localisation re-
sult [BB81] for the Springer resolution T ∗(G/B) →N and following the idea of
Kashiwara and Rouquier [KR08] to quantise the Hamiltonian reduction process
(which they applied to the symplectic resolution Hilbn(T ∗C) →(T ∗C)n/Sn given
by the punctual Hilbert scheme), one can consider also quantisations of hyper-
toric varieties Yδ(∆) →Y0(∆). These were considered in [MVdB98], and a certain
(micro)localisation was constructed in [BK12, BLPW12] (see also [Los12, Los22]).
That is, there is a sheaf Aℏof noncommutative, ℏ-adic algebras on Yδ(∆) whose
algebra of (C×-invariant) global sections [Aℏ(Yδ(∆))]C× equals the quantisation
of C[Y0(∆)].
These examples fit into the framework of quantisations of conical
symplectic resolutions in the sense of [BLPW16a, BLPW16b].
As suggested in [KR08], in the symplectic setting the localisation needs to be
upgraded to a microlocalisation in the sense of microdifferential operators with
homogenising parameter ℏ. To give a minimal example, in order to localise the
quantisation of T ∗C, the Weyl algebra, on all of T ∗C and not just on the pushfor-
ward to C (i.e. to quantise OT ∗C), we need to replace the differential operators DC
by their (ℏ-adic) deformation quantisation version. For instance when considering
the global sections, one may remove the ℏ-adic structure (which is too big in some
sense) by considering a certain C×-invariant structure.
Moreover, one may re-interpret the quantum Hamiltonian reduction to obtain
quantised hypertoric varieties in terms of BRST cohomology [Kuw15].
Vertex (super)algebras V can be viewed as chiral quantisations of affine Poisson
schemes [Ara12]. In good cases, they quantise the arc space (or ∞-jet scheme) of
the associated Poisson scheme ˜XV . In this work, we mostly focus on the underlying
Poisson variety XV = ( ˜XV )red.
Now, motivated by the 3d/2d-correspondence [Gai19, CG19, CCG19] and the 3d
Higgs branch conjecture [BF25] (which we comment upon below), in this work we
lift the quantisation-localisation of hypertoric varieties to vertex algebras. That is,
we construct a sheaf Vℏ
δ,∆of ℏ-adic vertex algebras in the sense of [AKM15, Li04]
on Yδ(∆) by quantum Hamiltonian reduction (BRST cohomology).

lowing [AKM23] (see also [CSYZ25]), we construct Vℏ

Theorem 5.10. The boundary hypertoric vertex operator superalgebra V (∆) is
simple, self-contragredient and of CFT-type. Its associated Poisson variety
XV (∆) ∼= Y0(∆)
recovers the affine hypertoric variety Y0(∆). In particular, V (∆) is quasi-lisse.
The quasi-lisseté (in the sense of [AK18]) follows because symplectic singularities
have finitely many symplectic leaves [Kal06].
By contrast, the C×-invariant global sections of the sheaf constructed in [Kuw21],
which we call minimal hypertoric vertex operator algebras and denote by Vmin(∆) in
this text, are in general not quasi-lisse. This is because the universal family ˜Yδ(∆)
does not have finitely many symplectic leaves to begin with.
We shall explain

Physical Background. The study of vertex (super)algebras associated with symplec-
tic singularities has been enriched by its connection to supersymmetric quantum
field theory, and the fruitfulness of this interaction continues to grow.
According to the 4d/2d-correspondence [BLL+15], it is possible to canonically
assign a vertex algebra to a 4d N = 2 superconformal quantum field theory. More-
over, the Higgs branch conjecture [BR18] states that the associated Poisson variety
of the vertex algebra arising from such a theory is isomorphic to the Higgs branch of
the theory, which is expected to be a symplectic singularity. One can then speculate
that at least in certain cases, the vertex algebras arising from 4d N = 2 theories
are global sections of sheaves of ℏ-adic vertex algebras, in the sense of [AKM15],
on the respective (resolved) Higgs branches. The case of pure type-A gauge theo-
ries motivated the construction of sheaves of ℏ-adic vertex algebras on the Hilbert
scheme of points given by two of the authors in [AKM23].
More recently, an analogue of the Higgs branch conjecture has been investigated
in a closely related physical context, boundary vertex algebras of topologically
twisted 3d N = 4 supersymmetric quantum field theories. Indeed, in what is called
the 3d/2d-correspondence, from any 3d N = 4 quantum field theory T one can
produce two cohomological topological field theories, the A- and B-twist, which
when put on a half-space support vertex algebras
V A(T )
and
V B(T ),
respectively, at their boundaries [Gai19, CG19, CCG19].
This can be thought
of as a nonunitary and generically logarithmic generalisation of the famous Chern-
Simons/Wess-Zumino-Witten correspondence [Wit89]. The parent 3d N = 4 theory
T has two distinguished branches of vacua, the Higgs MH(T ) and Coulomb branch
MH(T ), which are expected to be closely related to the boundary vertex algebras.
In the 3d setting, in good cases, both the Higgs and Coulomb branch are ex-
pected to be symplectic singularities (see, e.g., [Kam22, WY23] for recent surveys).
Mathematically, the Higgs branch MH(T ) is straightforwardly defined as a holo-
morphic symplectic quotient by the gauge group G [HKLR87]. The Coulomb branch
MC(T ), on the other hand, is much more difficult to construct. The celebrated
work of Braverman, Finkelberg and Nakajima [BFN18] gives a definition based on
the geometry of affine Grassmannians.
Now, the 3d Higgs branch conjecture [BF25] formulated by one of the authors
states that, under favourable circumstances (and with the right choice of boundary
conditions), the associated variety of the boundary vertex algebra V A(T ),
(9)
XV A(T ) ∼= MH(T )
recovers the Higgs branch MH(T ) of T . This also leads to the expectation that
the boundary vertex algebras V A(T ) are quasi-lisse.
The hypertoric vertex superalgebras V (∆) constructed in this work are the
boundary vertex superalgebras of 3d N = 4 gauge theories with abelian gauge
group G = (C×)M [BF25, BCDN23, Niu23]. Hence, Theorem 5.10 states:
Theorem. The Higgs branch conjecture holds for all 3d N = 4 gauge theories with
abelian gauge group and unimodular charge matrix ∆.
This conjecture was proved in [FS24] for the special case of minimal nilpo-
tent orbit closures for slN, which were identified with the associated varieties of
L1(pslN|N); see below.

VERTEX SUPERALGEBRAS FOR HYPERTORIC VARIETIES
5
Using symplectic duality (which reduces to Gale duality) and 3d mirror symme-
try, this also shows an analogous result for the Coulomb branch MC(T ) and the
B-twisted boundary vertex algebra V B(T ).
Free-Field Realisations and Screening Kernels. Given the construction of a vertex
algebra as global sections of a sheaf of ℏ-adic vertex algebras on some (smooth)
variety, one can obtain free-field realisations by restricting to nice open subsets.
Examples of such free-field embeddings are given in [Kuw21, AKM23] based on
local trivialisations of the defining Hamiltonian actions.
This picture is particularly elegant in the case of the symplectic resolutions
π: Yδ(∆) →Y0(∆) given by hypertoric varieties.
In fact, in [BF25, BCDN23]
explicit and rigorous free-field realisations for the hypertoric vertex operator su-
peralgebras V (∆) were obtained in terms of a “bosonisation” of the Weyl vertex
algebra Dch(T ∗V ) in the input of the BRST reduction,
Dch(T ∗V ) ,→Dch(T ∗(C×)N).
This reproduces the well-known embedding of the Weyl vertex algebra into a half-
lattice vertex algebra (or Heisenberg-lattice vertex algebra). It was shown that the
BRST cohomology can be easily performed at the level of the bosonised fields. As
a consequence, the vertex superalgebra V (∆) can be realised by applying screening
kernels to Dch(T ∗(C×)N−M) tensored with free fermions; see Proposition 3.18.
Now, in principle our sheaf construction should enable us to prove the ex-
pectation of [BF25] that these free fields can be identified with the local sec-
tions of the sheaves of ℏ-adic vertex superalgebras on openly embedded subsets
T ∗(C×)N−M ,→Yδ(∆); see our remarks in Section 5.4.
Symplectic Duality. One of the most intriguing aspects of the study of symplectic
singularities is that these come in pairs with many matching properties [BLPW16b].
Many examples of symplectic dual pairs are provided by the Higgs and Coulomb
branch MH(T ) and MC(T ) of the same 3d N = 4 theory T . This phenomenon is
closely related to 3d mirror symmetry in physics [IS96], which predicts the existence
of a dual theory T ∨that has Higgs and Coulomb branches swapped compared to T .
In this work, we explore how (some aspects of) symplectic duality can be upgraded
to vertex algebras in the special case of hypertoric varieties.
For hypertoric varieties, symplectic duality is perhaps best understood, where it
reduces to Gale duality [BLPW10, BLPW12]; see Section 2.4. Indeed, to any hy-
pertoric variety Y0(∆) one can associate another hypertoric variety, its symplectic
dual Y0(∆!). As main examples, we consider in this text the Gale dual symplec-
tic singularities Y0(∆) = Omin(slN) = {Z ∈slN | rk(Z) ≤1}, the minimal nilpotent
orbit closure of slN, and Y0(∆!) = XAN−1 = C2/(Z/NZ), the Kleinian (or du Val)
singularity of type AN−1; see below.
Physically, whilst Y0(∆) = MH(T ) is the Higgs branch of the 3d N = 4 super-
symmetric field theory T with (abelian) gauge group action defined by the datum ∆,
Y0(∆!) = MH(T ∨) is the Higgs branch of the dual theory T ∨defined by ∆!. By 3d
mirror symmetry, this is then isomorphic to the Coulomb branch MC(T ) of T ,
MH(T ) = Y0(∆)
↔
MC(T ) = MH(T ∨) = Y0(∆!).
In general, the Coulomb branch MC(T ) of a 3d N = 4 gauge theory T is difficult
to construct mathematically [BFN18], and the same is expected to be true for
the B-twisted boundary vertex algebras V B(T ), which (in analogy to the better
understood Higgs branch picture) should be understood as some kind of chiral
quantisations of the Coulomb branch. Consequently, mathematical constructions
of V B(T ) are very rare, unless they can be identified with the Higgs branch of a
mirror dual theory.

TOMOYUKI ARAKAWA, ANDREA E. V. FERRARI AND SVEN MÖLLER
For the special case of hypertoric varieties, a construction of V B(T ) was proposed
in [BCDN23], where an isomorphism V A(T ) ∼= V B(T ∨) was also proved. This was
possible because in this case we are in the fortunate position that
V A(T ) = V (∆)
↔
V B(T ) = V A(T ∨) = V (∆!).
In particular, we can construct V B(T ) (and the corresponding sheaf) in the same
way as V A(T ), namely with the localisation-quantisation approach for Hamiltonian
torus reductions pursued in this work. This is because the symplectic dual variety
of a hypertoric variety is again (by 3d mirror symmetry) a hypertoric variety.
Now, it is expected that many features of symplectic duality, here between Y0(∆)
and Y0(∆!), lift to the corresponding boundary vertex algebras, here V (∆) and
V (∆!). In this work, we explore some aspects of this.
One basic feature of symplectic duality is that the Lie algebra of torus Hamil-
tonian isometries of Y0(∆) is isomorphic to the Lie algebra of stability parameters
of Y0(∆!). It was claimed in [BF25, BCDN23] that these are reflected by CN−M
inner and noninner automorphisms of V (∆) and V (∆!), respectively, arising from
the inner automorphisms of the tensor factor Cℓ(ΠT ∗V ) in the input of the BRST
reduction. It was further pointed out in [BF25] (based on observations in [CCG19])
that whenever a subalgebra Cℓ⊂CN−M of the infinitesimal torus Hamiltonian
isometries of Y (∆!) extends to an slℓinfinitesimal Hamiltonian isometry, it is in
bijection with slℓnoninner automorphisms of V (∆!), see Proposition 5.16. In this
paper we complete this picture and show that these are themselves in bijection with
L1(slℓ) affine subalgebras of V (∆), see Proposition 5.15. This can be thought of
as a vertex operator superalgebra enhancement of the aforementioned symplectic
duality statement.

Characters. By using a vanishing result for nonzero cohomologies [BF25, Vor94]
(see Corollary 3.19) and the Euler-Poincaré principle, Theorem 4.1, we can com-
pute the characters of the boundary hypertoric vertex superalgebras V (∆) and of
their minimal versions Vmin(∆) using the Euler-Poincaré principle. The former co-
incide with the half-indices of the 3d N = 4 abelian gauge theories to which they
correspond via the 3d/2d-correspondence.
As the minimal hypertoric vertex algebras Vmin(∆) from [Kuw21] are not quasi-
lisse, not much can be concluded about the modular properties of their characters
on general grounds. We show that they can be written as infinite sums of partial
(or false) theta functions; see Proposition 7.5.
However, when the Vmin(∆) are extended to the quasi-lisse vertex operator su-
peralgebras V (∆), one can conclude that their (super)characters must satisfy a
modular linear differential equation [AK18, Li23]. In fact, we expect them to be
(quasi)modular forms (of mixed, nonnegative weights).
It is easy to show this for the supercharacters, which are of the simple form
schV (∆)(q) = η(q)2M,
where η(q) is the Dedekind η-function; see Proposition 7.2.
The characters take a more complicated form; see Proposition 7.7 and Proposi-
tion 7.8. We only explore their modular properties in the two aforementioned series
of examples. Concretely, in the case of the Kleinian singularity Y0(∆) = XAN−1,
we show in Proposition 7.12 that

Notation and Conventions. All vector spaces, algebras, Lie algebras, vertex
algebras, etc. are over the base field C unless otherwise noted.
Throughout, a
variety will be an integral, separated scheme of finite type over C.
Let G = (C×)M be a torus and V a G-module. As usual, we denote the G-
invariant elements of V by V G = {v ∈V | g · v = v for all g ∈G}.
For a
character δ ∈Hom(G, C×), we let V G,δ = {v ∈V | g · v = δ(g)v for all g ∈G}
denote the (G, δ)-semi-invariant elements. We also consider fractional characters
δ ∈Hom(G, C×) ⊗Z Q. In that case, V G,δ is zero unless δ ∈Hom(G, C×).
A matrix ∆is called unimodular if all the maximal minors take values in
{−1, 0, 1}.
Let ˆ⊗denote the completion of the tensor product ⊗C[[ℏ]] with respect to the
ℏ-adic topology.

2. Hypertoric and Quiver Varieties
We review hypertoric (or toric hyperkähler) varieties, the main geometric objects
of this text [HKLR87, BD00] (see also [HS02, PW07, Pro08]). They are a family of
conical symplectic singularities [Bea00] (and their resolutions) that can be realised
as Hamiltonian reduction of a symplectic vector space T ∗V = T ∗CN by the action
of an algebraic torus G = (C×)M. We also refer to [BK12, Kuw21] for a more
thorough introduction in a similar context.
Special cases of hypertoric varieties are quiver varieties [Nak94, Nak98] associ-
ated with quivers with abelian gauge nodes (see Appendix A). Examples of these
quiver hypertoric varieties include minimal nilpotent orbit closures for slN (see
Section 2.11) and Kleinian singularities of type AN−1 (see Section 2.12).
2.1. Conical Symplectic Resolutions. Hypertoric varieties are in particular
conical symplectic singularities that admit conical symplectic resolutions [Bea00,
BLPW16a, BLPW16b] (see also [Kam22]). Recall that a symplectic resolution is a
morphism π: X →X0 of complex algebraic varieties such that
(1) X is smooth and has a symplectic structure,
(2) X0 is affine, normal and has a Poisson structure,
(3) π is projective, birational and Poisson.
Moreover, a symplectic resolution is called conical if there are C×-actions on X0
and X, compatible via π, such that C× contracts X0 to a single point 0 ∈X0. We
assume that the symplectic form has weight 2 under the C×-action.


## Page 9


VERTEX SUPERALGEBRAS FOR HYPERTORIC VARIETIES
9
A conical symplectic resolution is Hamiltonian if there are Hamiltonian actions
of a torus T on X0 and X such that π is T-equivariant.
2.2. Hypertoric Varieties and Their Symplectic Resolutions. In the fol-
lowing, we introduce hypertoric varieties [HKLR87, BD00]. Most of the results
presented here can be found in [HS02] and [BK12], or in the survey [Pro08]. We
mostly follow the notation used in [Kuw21].

We recall some important properties of the hypertoric varieties Yδ(∆) from
[HS02, BK12]. If the stability parameter δ is in the interior of a maximal cone
of ∆(G, µ−1(0)), then the hypertoric variety Yδ(∆) is an orbifold (and coincides
with the geometric quotient T ∗V/G). In this case, we say that δ is generic. Yδ(∆)
is smooth if and only if δ is generic and ∆is unimodular. In that case, Yδ(∆) has
complex dimension 2(N −M). Recall that we say that the matrix ∆is unimodular
if every (M × M)-minor takes values in {−1, 0, 1}. Moreover, the moment map µ
is flat, and µ−1(0) is a reduced complete intersection in T ∗V .
Convention 2.4. Throughout the paper, we assume that the matrix ∆∈ZM×N
is surjective, unimodular and that δ ∈QM is a generic effective stability parameter.
The symplectic structure on T ∗V induces a symplectic structure on Yδ(∆), and
a Poisson structure on Y0(∆). The morphism π preserves these Poisson structures,
i.e. there is a homomorphism of Poisson algebras OY0(∆) →OYδ(∆). There is also
a conical C×-action on Yδ(∆) and Y0(∆) inherited from the linear scaling action
of C× on T ∗V . Overall, one can show that under the unimodularity assumption,
π: Yδ(∆) →Y0(∆) is a (conical and Hamiltonian) symplectic resolution.

2.4. Symplectic Duality (and 3d Mirror Symmetry). We briefly recall sym-
plectic duality for hypertoric varieties [BLPW16b]. See [Kam22, WY23] for recent
reviews. The closely related physical concept of 3d mirror symmetry, which we
shall comment upon even more briefly, was introduced in [IS96].
Very roughly, the (expected) statement of symplectic duality is that given some
symplectic resolution X →X0 one can find a dual symplectic resolution X! →X!
0,
with certain structures of X corresponding to certain other structures of X! and
such that (X!)! ∼= X. Most known examples of symplectic dual pairs (X, X!) come
from Higgs and Coulomb branches of 3d N = 4 theories.
While in full generality symplectic duality remains hypothetical, there are some
well-understood families of examples. In fact, an early instance of symplectic du-
ality is Gale duality of hypertoric varieties [BLPW10, BLPW12], which has been
thoroughly studied. Indeed, given a hypertoric variety, its symplectic dual is also
a hypertoric variety, namely the Gale dual hypertoric variety.
We now describe the Gale dual hypertoric variety. Recall the short exact se-
quences

are symplectic dual (here: Gale dual) symplectic resolutions, of dimension 2(N−M)
and dimension 2M, respectively, along with Hamiltonian C×-actions on each. As
discussed in Section 2.3, the stability parameter δ becomes the parameter defining
the C×-action on the Gale dual and vice versa for ϵ.

We mention that the two examples of hypertoric (quiver) varieties discussed in
Section 2.11 and Section 2.12 are a Gale dual pair, as we shall explain in more
detail there.
Finally, let us comment on 3d mirror symmetry in the context of 3d abelian
gauge theories. This is the statement that for any theory defined by the datum ∆,
there is a a mirror dual theory, defined by the datum ∆!, such that the Higgs branch
of one theory is isomorphic, as a hyperkähler variety, to the Coulomb branch of the
other theory, and vice versa (see also Section 5.2). Thus, the symplectic resolutions
Yδ(∆) →Y0(∆) and Yϵ(∆!) →Y0(∆!) are the Higgs and Coulomb branches and the
Coulomb and Higgs branches of the theories defined by ∆and ∆!, respectively.
2.5. Poisson Isomorphisms. We continue with a few remarks on holomorphic
symplectic (and therefore Poisson) isomorphisms between hypertoric varieties. Let
(∆1, E1) be a pair of unimodular (M×N)- and (N×(N−M))-matrices, respectively,
entering the above short exact sequence (1). Recall that ∆1 and E1 are defined up
to a choice of basis, and different choices lead to isomorphic hypertoric varieties.
As we now explain, there are further operations that lead to Poisson isomorphism
of hypertoric varieties. See, e.g., Proposition 1.3.2 in [Pro08], or the classification
in [Nag21], Theorem 4.2.
Remark 2.5. Let (∆2, E2) be another pair of matrices that can be obtained from
(∆1, E1) by multiplying some of the columns of ∆1 and some of the rows of E1
by an overall sign, or by swapping columns of ∆1 and rows of E1. Then Y0(∆1) is
isomorphic as a holomorphic symplectic (and therefore Poisson) variety to Y0(∆2),
and Y0((∆1)!) is isomorphic as a holomorphic symplectic (and therefore Poisson)
variety to Y0((∆2)!). Thus, the data defining the hypertoric variety Y0(∆) as a
Poisson variety can equally well be taken to be the set of hyperplanes determined
by the rows of E in RN−M, upon fixing the standard realisation ZN−M ⊂RN−M
without orientation.
Without loss of generality, we can further restrict the assumptions on ∆and E
slightly. We shall use this in Proposition 2.12, for example. Recall that, as the map
defined by ∆is surjective, it cannot have any zero row.

that there exist b ∈ZN−M and d+, d−∈QN such that ∆d+ = ∆d−= δ and
d+
i > 0 ⇒(Eb)i > 0,
d+
i < 0 ⇒(Eb)i < 0,
as well as
d−
i > 0 ⇒−(Eb)i > 0,
d−
i < 0 ⇒−(Eb)i < 0.
Indeed, in that case, the monomials md+ | W b and md−| W −b, which shows that
W b and W −b are in the ideal (md | ∆d = δ) ⊂I(Z) of C[T ∗V ].
To prove the rephrased assertion, fix a solution d ∈QN of ∆d = δ, which always
exists as ∆has full rank. Then for any r ∈Q and any vector b ∈ZM−N, by the fact
that ∆E = 0, ∆(d + rEb) = δ. By Remark 2.6, we may assume that E does not
have any zero row. Hence, there exists b ∈ZM−N \ {0} such that Eb ∈ZN ⊂QN
has no zero entries. Then, for large enough r ∈Q, d+ := d + rEb has entries with
the same sign as Eb, and d−:= d −rEb has entries with the same sign as −Eb.
This proves the assertion.
□
Remark 2.13. In view of Lemma 4.5, one would want to prove a slightly stronger
version of Proposition 2.12, where one additionally requires that the solutions d+
and d−of ∆d = δ be minimal in the sense of Remark 2.11. In other words, we
would like to show that it is possible to find sets C, C′ ∈C and a vector b ∈ZM−N
such that mC | W b and mC′ | W −b.
This is likely true under no further (or only very mild) assumptions on the
stability parameter δ ∈QM, but we do not verify this here.
We can slightly reformulate the problem to finding two sets C, C′ ∈C such that
the corresponding minimal solutions d, d′ of ∆d = δ satisfy that d and −d′ lie in
the same closed orthant of QN, which means that dj ≤0 if and only if −d′
j ≤0
and dj ≥0 if and only if −d′
j ≥0 for all j = 1, . . . , N. Then, v := d −d′ lies in
the same orthant and also in the kernel of ∆and hence in the column space of E.
By scaling, it is of the form v = Eb for some b ∈ZM−N that then satisfies the
conditions in the proof of Proposition 2.12.

VERTEX SUPERALGEBRAS FOR HYPERTORIC VARIETIES
23
3. Vertex Operator Superalgebras for Hypertoric Varieties
In this section, we consider two families of vertex operator (super)algebras by
chiralising the construction of the (singular) affine hypertoric varieties Y0(∆) by
Hamiltonian reduction.
However, there are anomalies present that prevent the
BRST differential from closing. Depending on the choice of anomaly cancellation,
we call these minimal hypertoric vertex operator algebras Vmin(∆) and boundary
hypertoric vertex operator superalgebras V (∆).
The former were first defined and considered in [Kuw21] and use Heisenberg ver-
tex operator algebras for anomaly cancellation (see also [NS25, Sas25] for the study
of some examples, and [CSYZ25] for the generalisation to nonabelian quivers), while
the latter [BF25, BCDN23, Niu23] use free fermions (see also [Yos23, FS24, Sas25]
for the study of some examples, and [AKM23] for fermionic anomaly cancellation
appearing in a different context).
As a consequence, the boundary hypertoric vertex operator superalgebras are
infinite-index conformal extensions of the minimal hypertoric vertex operator al-
gebras (times a Heisenberg vertex operator algebra). Here, we give a systematic
study of these fermionic extensions. Based on this, we shall determine the asso-
ciated variety of the boundary hypertoric vertex operator superalgebras in Sec-
tion 5.2. We shall see that they are, in contrast to the minimal hypertoric vertex
operator algebras, chiral quantisations of the underlying affine hypertoric varieties,
i.e. XV (∆) = Y0(∆). This also means that they are quasi-lisse, while the minimal
hypertoric vertex operator algebras in general are not.
Another interesting phenomenon related to these fermionic extensions, at least
in the examples that we study, is that the characters of the hypertoric vertex
operator algebras are upgraded from partial (or false) theta functions, which are
not modular, to (quasi)modular forms, as we shall discuss in Section 7.
Examples of the vertex operator superalgebra constructions in this section cor-
responding to the hypertoric varieties studied in Section 2.11 and Section 2.12 will
be considered in Section 6.
While the vertex operator superalgebras V (∆) in this section are analogues (or,
more precisely, chiral quantisations) of the coordinate ring C[Y0(∆)] of the affine
and singular hypertoric varieties Y0(∆), in Section 4 we shall also present a vertex
algebraic analogue of the smooth versions Yδ(∆). More precisely, we define certain
sheaves Vℏ
δ,∆of (ℏ-adic) vertex operator superalgebras over the hypertoric varieties
Yδ(∆). By contrast, the sheaves constructed in [Kuw21] using minimal anomaly
cancellation live over the universal family ˜Yδ(∆) of Poisson deformations of the
hypertoric variety rather than the variety itself.
In Section 4.3 we compare these two constructions. Classically, the global sec-
tions OYδ(∆)(Yδ(∆)) of the structure sheaf OYδ(∆) coincide with the coordinate ring
of Y0(∆), C[Y0(∆)] ∼= OYδ(∆)(Yδ(∆)). Using the faithfulness theorem in [ADS26],
we shall prove a vertex algebraic analogue of this statement.
3.1. Free-Field Vertex Operator Superalgebras. For the construction of the
hypertoric vertex operator superalgebras by quantum Hamiltonian reduction, we
need the Weyl and Clifford vertex operator (super)algebras, also called βγ-system
and bc-system, respectively.
Let V = CN for N ∈Z≥0 be a finite-dimensional vector space. As in Section 2.2,
consider the cotangent bundle T ∗V = V ⊕V ∗equipped with the natural symplectic
form. Recall that x1, . . . , xN and y1, . . . , yN are the standard coordinate functions
of T ∗V ; they are Darboux coordinates with respect to the symplectic form. The co-
ordinate ring C[T ∗V ] ∼= C[x1, . . . , xN, y1, . . . , yN] is a Poisson algebra with Poisson
bracket {yi, xj} = δij and {xi, xj} = {yi, yj} = 0 for i, j = 1, . . . , N.

3.2. Minimal and Boundary Hypertoric Vertex Operator Superalgebras.
In the following, we define the two version of the hypertoric vertex operator (su-

and the larger boundary hypertoric vertex operator superalgebras V (∆).

Definition 3.5 (Minimal Hypertoric Vertex Operator Superalgebra). We define

Also Vmin(∆) = H•(Cmin, d) = H0(Cmin, d) satisfies a vanishing of nonzero co-
homologies. In [Kuw21], this vertex operator algebra is simply called hypertoric
vertex operator algebra. We shall further explain how to describe V (∆) as a (con-
formal) lattice extension of Vmin(∆) ⊗πT E, both vertex operator (super)algebras
of central charge c = −2M.
3.3. Vanishing Theorem. In this section, we study the minimal and hypertoric
vertex operator (super)algebras Vmin(∆) and V (∆) concretely, i.e. we compute the
relative BRST cohomologies. Along the way, we also prove a vanishing theorem for
nonzero cohomologies, i.e. H∞/2+n(g, Mmin) = {0} and H∞/2+n(g, M) = {0} for
all n ̸= 0 [Vor94] (see also [BF25]).

Remark 3.7 (Parity). As the lattice L is in general not even, but integral, it
contains vectors x of norm ⟨x, x⟩/2 ∈Z and those of norm ⟨x, x⟩/2 ∈Z + 1/2.
Fock modules (and the submodules obtained by applying screening kernels) corre-
sponding to the former have even parity, those corresponding to the latter have odd
parity. As the −ρi + σi are isotropic, all of this only depends on the τi. In other
words, Fock modules with momentum · · · + PN
i=1 niτi have parity (−1)n1+···+nN .
Cf. Remark 7.4 below.

generate symmetric bilinear form, isometric to, e.g., i⊥/i ∼= ⟨ρ⟩C ∼= ⟨−ρ + σ + τ⟩C.

The vanishing result states that all relative cohomologies (even the zeroth) vanish
unless λ ∈h⊥. In that case, the Fock module survives, but with a reduction in
rank from h to h⊥, i.e. from 3N to 3N −2M.
From this, using the commutativity of the diagrams in Section 3.3.1, zoomed in
to the level of Fock modules, we can also determine the BRST cohomology of the
direct summands of Mmin ⊗πT E and M. We can then use this in Section 3.3.5 to
compute the BRST cohomology of Mmin ⊗πT E and M.

reduction are conformal extensions

be obtained from the above modules by summing over the lattices K and L in h,
respectively.
3.3.5. Vanishing Theorem. We apply the vanishing result, Proposition 3.14, to the
free-field vertex operator (super)algebras Mmin ⊗πT E and M in order to obtain the
minimal and boundary hypertoric vertex operator(super)algebras Vmin(∆) ⊗πT E
and V (∆), respectively. We shall see that they are certain simple-current extensions
of Heisenberg and singlet vertex operator algebras.

dual pair in V (∆), and the latter is a finite simple-current extension of the former.
This observation is, before applying the screening kernels, mirrored by Re-
mark 3.12. K⊥⊕J⊥⊂L⊥does not have finite index (i.e. the same rank) in general,
but we can consider the infinite-order extension K⊥of K⊥so that K⊥⊕J⊥is a
finite-index (same-rank) sublattice of L⊥. We forego a more detailed discussion
here, and rather refer to the examples in Section 6.

3.4. Simplicity and Simple-Current Extensions. In Section 3.3 we showed
that the minimal hypertoric vertex operator algebra V (∆) and the boundary hyper-
toric vertex operator superalgebras Vmin(∆) are simple-current extensions of tensor
products of singlet and Heisenberg vertex operator algebras (see also [BCDN23,
Niu23]). This implies:
Proposition 3.22. The minimal and boundary hypertoric (vertex)operator super-
algebras Vmin(∆) and V (∆) are simple vertex operator (super)algebras.
Proof. Both the singlet vertex operator algebra M(2) [Ada03] and the Heisenberg
vertex operator algebra are simple, and hence so are tensor products of them. The
vertex operator (super)algebras in question are then simple-current extensions of
such a tensor product. Hence, they must also be simple. The simplicity of simple-
current extensions is well-established in the literature in the finite-index case, but
the argument carries over to countable extensions without complication.
□
The simplicity of Vmin(∆) was also proved in [Kuw21] using a commutant de-
scription [Lin09].
The simplicity of the boundary hypertoric vertex operator superalgebras also
follows from [BG26]. They show that a cohomological vertex operator superalgebra
such as V (∆) is weakly graded-unitary because M = Dch(T ∗V ) ⊗Cℓ(ΠT ∗V ) has
this property, which is preserved by the relative BRST cohomology in good cases.
Moreover, V (∆) is of CFT-type by construction. Then, by Remark 2.18 in [BG26],
V (∆) is simple, self-contragredient and of CFT-type. The last statement also holds
for Vmin(∆). We summarise this is the following proposition:
Proposition 3.23. The minimal and boundary hypertoric (vertex)operator super-
algebras Vmin(∆) and V (∆) are simple, self-contragredient and of CFT type.
The L0-grading (see also Corollary 3.27 below) of Vmin(∆) and V (∆) takes values
in (1/2)Z≥0, but in the case of V (∆) there is in general no relation (“correct
statistics”) between the parity and integrality of the L0-grading. These properties
are directly inherited from M.

4. BRST Cohomology Sheaves over Symplectic Resolutions
In this section, following the fermionic construction developed in [AKM23], we
define a sheaf Vℏ
δ,∆of ℏ-adic (i.e. microlocalised) vertex operator superalgebras
over the smooth (projective) hypertoric variety Yδ(∆) by quantum Hamiltonian
reduction. In contrast to [Kuw21], this sheaf will be defined over the hypertoric
variety (or a nilpotent thickening of it) rather than over its universal family of
Poisson deformations ˜Yδ(∆) (cf. Section 2.8 and Section 2.9).
This is due to a
different anomaly cancellation, replacing the Heisenberg vertex operator algebra
with free fermions (see also [Niu23]).
In the end, we will see in Section 4.3 that the global sections Vℏ
δ,∆(Yδ(∆)), after
removing the microlocalisation parameter ℏby considering the S-invariant structure
for a certain action by the torus S = C×, coincide with the boundary hypertoric
vertex operator superalgebra V (∆). That is, we show that the natural map
V (∆) →

δ,∆(Yδ(∆))

is an isomorphism. To prove this, we use a faithfulness theorem from [ADS26].
Similarly, the global sections of the sheaf over ˜Yδ(∆) defined in [Kuw21] reduce to
the minimal hypertoric vertex operator algebra Vmin(∆), as was shown there.

Since V (∆) is simple, the map is injective. We view ˜V δ(∆) as a module for V (∆).
In the following, we show that the map is an isomorphism using the faithfulness
theorem in [ADS26].
Theorem 4.1 (Global Sections). As vertex operator superalgebras,
V (∆) ∼= ˜V δ(∆).
The rest of the section is dedicated to the proof of this theorem.
First, we recall the following truncation condition on the L0-weight grading for
modules of a conformal vertex algebra V . A V -module M is called lower-truncated
if for any m ∈M there exists an integer k ∈Z>0 such that for any homogeneous
elements v1, . . . , vs and integers i1, . . . , is satisfying Ps
j=1(−wt(vj) + 1 + ij) > k,
it follows that v1
(i1) . . . vs
(is) = 0. This is satisfied in particular when the L0-grading
on M is bounded from below.
We then recall the notions of (lower-truncated) vertex A-superalgebras and A-
loop modules from [Bor01b] (see also [ADS26]). They allow us to localise vertex
superalgebras and their modules by weight-0 elements, in analogy to the localisation
of rings and their modules.
Let V be a Q≥0-graded (by L0-weights) conformal vertex superalgebra. Then
the even weight-zero space R := V0 ∩V ¯0 forms a unital, commutative, associative
C-algebra under the (−1)-st mode. Let A be a subring of R. Then V can be viewed
as an A-loop module and hence as vertex A-superalgebra. In that situation, given


## Page 54

TOMOYUKI ARAKAWA, ANDREA E. V. FERRARI AND SVEN MÖLLER
a multiplicative system S ⊂A, we may define the localisation VS of V by S, which
is then a vertex AS-superalgebra.
Similarly, in the above situation, if M is a (lower-truncated) module over the
vertex superalgebra V , then M is naturally a (lower-truncated) A-loop module. If
M is lower-truncated, then for a multiplicative system S ⊂A we may again define
the localisation MS of M by S, which is a lower-truncated AS-loop module.
The main tool in this section will be the following result from [ADS26].
Proposition 4.2 (Faithfulness Theorem). Let V be a vertex (super)algebra equipped
with two conformal structures determined by conformal elements ω1 and ω0. Sup-
pose that V is Q≥0-graded with respect to both conformal elements. Let f, g ∈V be
two even elements such that
(a) L1
0f = 0 and L0
0g = 0,
(b) L0
nf = 0 for all n ∈Z>0,
(c) L0
0f = λf for some λ ∈Q>0, which we write as λ = p/q with p, q ∈Z>0
and (p, q) = 1,
(d) (f nq)(np−1)gnq1 = cn1 for some constant cn ̸= 0 for all sufficiently large
n ∈Z≥0.
In particular, we may consider the localisation Vf of V by f (which is in V0 ∩V ¯0
with respect to ω1) or Vg by g (which is in V0 ∩V ¯0 with respect to ω0). Similarly,
given a lower-truncated V -module M (for both ω1 and ω0), we may consider the
localisations Mf and Mg. Then, the following assertions hold:
(1) Let M be a lower-truncated V -module with respect to both ω1 and ω0. Sup-
pose that Mf = {0} and Mg = {0}. Then already M = {0}.
(2) Let M ′ be another such lower-truncated V -module and let ϕ: M →M ′ be a
homomorphism such that the induced maps Mf →M ′
f and Mg →M ′
g are
injective or surjective. Then ϕ itself is injective or surjective, respectively.
(3) If Vf and Vg are simple, then V itself is simple.
In this work, we shall only need to use the surjectivity in part (2) of the theorem.

5. Properties of Hypertoric Vertex Operator Superalgebras
In this section, we study the main properties of the hypertoric vertex operator
superalgebras V (∆) constructed in Section 3. Most importantly, we show that the
associated variety XV (∆) recovers the underlying affine hypertoric variety Y0(∆).
The modularity of the characters will be studied in Section 7 below.
5.1. Some Bosonic Fields. In the following, we describe some even fields of the
hypertoric vertex operator superalgebra V (∆). We shall use them in Section 5.2 to
study the associated variety of V (∆).
Recall the description of the coordinate ring C[Y0(∆)] = C[µ−1(0)]G. In par-
ticular, Proposition 2.8 provides elements Ji for i = 1, . . . , N −M and W a for
a ∈ZN−M, which generate C[Y0(∆)]. In fact, a finite subset of these elements
suffices.
Now, by replacing the coordinates xj, yj for j = 1, . . . , N by the fields of the
same name and products with normally ordered products, we obtain in particular
the following even fields in Dch(T ∗V ) ⊂M ⊂˜C:

5.2. Associated Variety. We prove that the associated variety XV (∆) of the
boundary hypertoric vertex operator superalgebra V (∆) constructed in Section 3
recovers the affine and singular hypertoric variety Y0(∆), XV (∆) ∼= Y0(∆). This
also proves the 3d Higgs branch conjecture [BF25] for a large class of boundary
vertex operator superalgebras. In other words, V (∆) is a chiral quantisation of the
coordinate ring C[Y0(∆)] of Y0(δ). In particular, V (∆) is quasi-lisse, in contrast to
the minimal hypertoric vertex operator algebra constructed in [Kuw21].
We recall some relevant definitions. For any vertex superalgebra V , there is a
decreasing filtration V = F0(V ) ⊃F1(V ) ⊃. . . , and we can consider the corre-
sponding associated graded object grF (V ) := L∞
n=0 Fn(V )/Fn+1(V ), which carries
the structure of a vertex Poisson algebra [Li05]. The lowest-degree component
RV := F0(V )/F1(V ) = V/F1(V ) = V/V(−2)V
is the C2-algebra [Zhu96], a Poisson superalgebra with the (super-commutative
and associative) product induced from the (−1)-st vertex algebra product and the
Poisson bracket from the 0-th one. RV generates grF (V ) as a differential algebra,
i.e. there is a surjection ϕ: J∞RV ↠grF (V ) from the arc space J∞RV of RV .
Often, this induces an isomorphism ϕred : J∞(RV )red ∼= grF (V )red.
Following [Ara12], one then defines the associated variety XV of V as the affine
Poisson variety
XV := Specm(RV ) = Spec((RV )red),
where (RV )red = RV /N is the reduced algebra obtained as the quotient of RV
by its nilradical N. A vertex superalgebra V is called chiral quantisation of an
(affine) Poisson variety X, if X ∼= XV . In other words (assuming that ϕred is an
isomorphism), V can be viewed as a quantisation of the arc space J∞X.
If the associated Poisson variety XV is a symplectic variety (in the sense of
having only finitely many symplectic leaves), then V is called quasi-lisse [AK18].
In the context of the 3d/2d-correspondence [Gai19, CG19, CCG19], given a 3d
N = 4 supersymmetric field theory T , one can associate with it the A-twisted (or
H-twisted) boundary vertex operator superalgebra V A(T ), which is thought to live
on the boundary of the topological A-twist of T . Further, one can extract from the
theory T the Higgs branch MH(T ), a certain symplectic variety appearing in the
moduli space of vacuum expectation values of T . Mathematically, the Higgs branch
MH(T ) is defined as a holomorphic symplectic quotient [HKLR87] by the gauge
group G (see, e.g., [Kam22, WY23] for recent surveys). Often, this is a symplectic
singularity in the sense of [Bea00]. The (3d version of the) Higgs branch conjecture
[BF25] states that in good cases, the associated variety of V A(T )
(9)
XV A(T ) ∼= MH(T )
recovers the Higgs branch MH(T ) of T . This also leads to the expectation that
the vertex operator superalgebras V A(T ) are quasi-lisse.
Hence, one of the key properties to study when mathematically constructing
the boundary vertex operator superalgebra V A(T ) is its associated variety.
In
Theorem 5.10, we verify the Higgs branch conjecture for 3d N = 4 supersymmetric
field theories with abelian gauge group G = (C×)M, i.e. when V A(T ) = V (∆) and

MH(T ) = Y0(∆). That is, we prove that XV (∆) ∼= Y0(∆) as Poisson varieties. In
particular, V (∆) is quasi-lisse.
We remark that a proof of the conjecture for the special case of ∆= (1, . . . , 1)
was already given in [FS24], where V (∆) was identified (for N ̸= 2) with the simple
affine vertex operator superalgebra L1(pslN|N). We shall return to this example in
Section 6.1.

Lemma 5.9. The conformal vector ω of V (∆), and its summands ωmin and ωT E,
are mapped to nilpotent elements in the C2-algebra RV (∆) = V (∆)/C2(V (∆)).
Proof. The lattice vertex operator superalgebra VJ⊥associated with the integral,
positive-definite lattice J⊥⊂T E is C2-cofinite. Hence, the image of ωT E in RVJ⊥
is nilpotent (see Lemma 4.11 in [AM24]). In particular, the image of ωT E in RV (∆)
is nilpotent.

But then (xiϕi)(yiψi) is mapped to the product of these odd elements, which is
also nilpotent. Hence, all summands in the images of ωmin and ω in RV (∆) are
nilpotent.
□

As the hypertoric varieties considered here are symplectic singularities in the

have finitely many symplectic leaves. Thus, by definition, we can conclude:
Corollary 5.11. V (∆) is quasi-lisse.
5.3. Automorphisms and Symplectic Duality. Recall from Section 2.4 that
given a hypertoric variety Y0(∆) associated with a unimodular matrix ∆, one can
always obtain a symplectic (or Gale) dual variety Y0(∆!). This implies that we
can consider pairs of vertex operator superalgebras, V (∆) and V (∆!), such as the
two examples studied in Section 6. By Theorem 5.10, these vertex operator su-
peralgebras are chiral quantisations of Y0(∆) and Y0(∆!), respectively. Thus, these
vertex operator superalgebras can be thought of as symplectic dual pairs of vertex
operator superalgebras.4
We discussed in Section 2.4 that Y0(∆) and Y0(∆!) are proved to have certain
properties swapped. For instance, the Lie algebra of (infinitesimal) torus symplectic
symmetries of one is in correspondence with the Lie algebra of stability parameters
of the other. This fact is reflected in automorphisms of V (∆) and V (∆!).
Proposition 5.12 ([BF25, BCDN23]). The weight-1 fields τ E

The proof is elementary. The weight-1 fields τE

zero modes, however, are annihilated and therefore preserve its kernel. Hence, they
descend to noninner automorphisms of V (∆).

To summarise, it follows from the above results that the symplectic dual vertex
operator superalgebras V (∆) and V (∆!) have certain (infinitesimal) inner and non-
inner automorphisms of A-type swapped. Moreover, the Cartan elements of these
Lie algebras reflect the isomorphism between the Lie algebra of infinitesimal torus
isometries and the Lie algebra of stability parameters of the two symplectic dual
varieties Y0(∆) and Y0(∆!).
Thus, these inner and noninner automorphisms can be thought of as a refinement,
at the level of chiral quantisations, of this basic symplectic duality statement.
Remark 5.17. Not all slℓ-type noninner automorphisms arise in this way. As an
example, consider ∆= (1), which gives for V (∆) the symplectic fermion vertex op-
erator superalgebra (see Section 3.3.2). Although it does not satisfy the conditions
of the above results, it has an sl2 noninner automorphism group.
Examples will be discussed in Section 6.
5.4. A Remark on Free-Field Realisations. Recall that the free-field realisa-
tion of V (∆) obtained in Section 3.3.1 relies on a vertex operator superalgebra
embedding
Dch(T ∗V ) ⊗Cℓ(ΠT ∗V ) ,→Dch(T ∗(C×)N) ⊗Cℓ(ΠT ∗V ),
which is a chiralisation of the embedding T ∗(C×)N ,→T ∗V . If we perform the
torus symplectic reduction on T ∗(C×)N instead of T ∗V , with (abusing notation)
µ−1(0) ⊂T ∗(C×)N the inverse image of the moment map, we obtain
U := µ−1(0)//G ∼= T ∗(C×)N−M.
It was shown in [BF25] that under our assumptions on ∆there exists a generic
effective stability parameter δ∆∈QM such that U can be identified with an openly
embedded subset
U ,→Yδ∆(∆).
It should follow from our sheaf construction that the free-field realisation obtained
in Section 3.3.1 can equally be obtained by localising our sheaf of ℏ-adic vertex
operator superalgebras to U ⊂Yδ∆(∆), and taking global sections; we leave this to
future work.
Also, note that there are two ways to obtain an embedding T ∗C× ,→T ∗C, by
removing either the zero element in the basis or the fibre. It is then in principle
possible to consider 2N embeddings T ∗(C×)N ,→T ∗V . It was further shown in
[BF25] that some of the resulting free-field realisations are better suited to manifest
the noninner automorphisms studied in Section 5.3. Others are better suited to find
free-field realisations based on localisations of the form
T ∗CN−M−L × T ∗(C×)L ,→T ∗V
for 1 ≤L ≤N −M.

6. Examples
In this section, we discuss two families of examples of (quiver) boundary hy-
pertoric vertex operator superalgebras V (∆), those associated with the minimal
nilpotent orbit closures for slN (see Section 2.11) and those associated with the
Kleinian singularities of type AN−1 (see Section 2.12). The minimal hypertoric
vertex operator algebras Vmin(∆) were already described in Section 10 of [Kuw21].
The extension to the boundary vertex operator superalgebra in the first example
was first treated in [FS24] (relying on results of [AM21]), where the associated
variety was computed using alternative methods. Some further partial results on
vertex operator superalgebras were obtained in [Yos23, Sas25]. Here, we focus in
particular on the fermionic extension picture developed in Section 3.
Recall from Proposition 3.23 that the vertex operator (super)algebras Vmin(∆)
and V (∆) are simple, self-contragredient and of CFT-type and from Corollary 5.11
that the V (∆) are quasi-lisse. The characters will be described in Section 7. We
also mention that the vertex operator superalgebras V (∆) are (1/2)Z≥0-graded
by L0-eigenvalues, but there is in general no relation between the parity and the
integrality of these eigenvalues.
6.1. Minimal Nilpotent Orbit Closure for slN. We fix N ∈Z>0, M = 1, the
(1 × N)-matrix ∆= (1, . . . , 1) and the corresponding (N × (N −1))-matrix E as
in Section 2.11. See Appendix A, and in particular Figure 4, for the corresponding
quiver realisation.
We summarise the properties of the hypertoric vertex operator (super)algebras
and their identifications with affine vertex operator (super)algebras given in [Kuw21,
FS24] (see also [AP14, AM17b]).
Proposition 6.1. For all N ∈Z>0, the minimal hypertoric vertex operator algebra
Vmin(∆) is simple, self-contragredient, of CFT-type and central charge c = −N −1.
For N ≥3, it is isomorphic to Vmin(∆) ∼= L−1(slN), the simple quotient of the
affine vertex operator algebra for slN at level −1. For N ≥4, the associated variety
of Vmin(∆) is given by Smin(slN), the closure of the unique minimal Dixmier sheet
containing the minimal nilpotent orbit Omin(slN).
For N ∈Z>0, the boundary hypertoric vertex operator superalgebra V (∆) is sim-
ple, self-contragredient, of CFT-type (and Z≥0-graded) and central charge c = −2.
It is quasi-lisse and its associated variety is given by XV (∆) ∼= Y0(∆) ∼= Omin(slN),
the minimal nilpotent orbit closure of slN. Moreover, for N = 1 and N ≥3, it is
isomorphic to V (∆) ∼= L1(pslN|N) ∼= L−1(pslN|N), the simple quotient of the affine
vertex operator superalgebra for pslN|N at level ±1.
The associated variety statement for V (∆) holds for all N ≥1, provided one
identifies the minimal nilpotent orbit closure with a point for N = 1.
We describe a few special cases for small N.
For N = 1, as described in
Section 3.3.2, Vmin(∆) is isomorphic to the singlet vertex operator algebra M(2)
[Wan98b], and the associated variety is the affine line C with trivial Poisson struc-
ture. On the other hand, V (∆) ∼= SF is isomorphic to the symplectic fermion vertex
operator superalgebra, of central charge c = −2, which can be identified (somewhat
trivially) with L±1(psl1|1).
For N = 2, V (∆) is isomorphic to the simple rectangular W-algebra of sl4 at
level −5/4 [CKLR19], which is a conformal extension of L−1(sl2).
In the following, for N ≥3, we describe how V (∆) ∼= L1(pslN|N) can be under-
stood as a simple-current extension of Vmin(∆) ⊗πT E ∼= L−1(slN) ⊗π⊗(N−1), as
explained in Section 3 in the general case. Some aspects of this were also treated
in [AM21] for this particular example.

6.2. Kleinian Singularity of Type AN−1. We then consider the symplectic dual
example to the previous one. That is, we fix N ∈Z>0, M = N−1, the ((N−1)×N)-
matrix ∆and the (N × 1)-matrix E = (1, . . . , 1)t as in Section 2.12. Again, see
Appendix A, and in particular Figure 5, for the corresponding quiver realisation.
We summarise the properties of the hypertoric vertex operator (super)algebras
and, in the minimal case, their identifications with certain W-algebras [FS04] given
in [Kuw21]. See, Remark 2.14 and [Nag21, Kuw21] for details regarding the (finite)
Namikawa-Weyl group W.
Proposition 6.2. For N ≥2, the minimal hypertoric vertex operator algebra
Vmin(∆) is simple, self-contragredient, of CFT-type and central charge c = −2N +1.
After taking invariants under the Namikawa-Weyl group W, it becomes isomorphic
to Vmin(∆)W ∼= W−N+1(slN, fsub), the simple quotient of the subregular W-algebra
for slN at level −N +1. The associated variety of Vmin(∆)W is given by the Slodowy
slice Sfsub ⊂sl∗
N.
For N ≥2, the boundary hypertoric vertex operator superalgebra V (∆) is simple,
self-contragredient, of CFT-type (and 1
2Z≥0-graded) and central charge c = −2N+2.
It is quasi-lisse and its associated variety is given by XV (∆) ∼= Y0(∆) ∼= XAN−1, the
Kleinian singularity of type AN−1, which is isomorphic to Sfsub ∩N, the Slodowy
slice intersected with the nilpotent cone of slN
The case N = 1 could be considered as well, but is is somewhat trivial as
M = 0, so no reduction is performed at all (cf. Remark 2.6). Correspondingly,
Vmin(∆) ∼= Dch(T ∗C) is the Weyl vertex algebra of central charge c = −1, and

7. Characters and Modularity
In this section, using the Euler-Poincaré principle, we compute the characters and
supercharacters of the minimal Vmin(∆) and boundary hypertoric vertex operator
(super)algebras V (∆) constructed in Section 3 and study their modular properties.
By Theorem 4.1, these are also the characters of the global sections of the corre-
sponding sheaves constructed in Section 4 over the smooth hypertoric varieties. We
will see that in the boundary case the supercharacters (and conjecturally also the
characters) are quasimodular. One the other hand, in the minimal case, they are
only partial (or false) theta functions.
7.1. General Expression. We recall from Section 3.2 the cohomological definition
of the hypertoric vertex operator (super)algebras
Vmin(∆) = H•(Cmin, d)
and
V (∆) = H•(C, d).

7.2. Boundary Supercharacter. The case of the supercharacter of the boundary
vertex operator superalgebra is the easiest. Here, the denominator and numerator
of the fraction in Proposition 7.1 cancel and we simply obtain:
Proposition 7.2. The supercharacter of the boundary hypertoric vertex operator
superalgebra V (∆) is
schV (∆)(q) = η(q)2M,
which only depends on the rank M of the torus G.
Example 7.3. For N = M = 1 and ∆= (1), we have seen in Section 3.3.2 that
V (∆) = SF is the symplectic fermion vertex operator superalgebra (with two strong
generators of L0-weight 1), whose supercharacter is schV (∆)(q) = schSF(q) = η(q)2.
More generally, the supercharacters for N ≥3, M = 1 and ∆= (1, . . . , 1),
corresponding to the minimal nilpotent orbit closures for slN, in which case the
boundary hypertoric vertex operator algebra is V (∆) ∼= L1(pslN|N) [FS24] (see
also Section 6.1), were already stated in [AM21].
As we showed in Corollary 5.11 that V (∆) is quasi-lisse, it follows from [Li23] (cf.
[AK18]) that the supercharacter of V (∆) satisfies a certain modular linear differen-
tial equation. It is expected that many quasi-lisse vertex operator(super) algebras
have (super)characters that belong to vector-valued modular forms of weight 0 for
SL2(Z) that may have log(q)-terms in other components. Often, this means that
they are weakly holomorphic (quasi)modular forms of mixed nonnegative weights
for some congruence subgroup and possibly a character (see, e.g., [AKM23], for
another example).
Indeed, the supercharacter schV (∆)(q) = η2M(q) is modular of weight M for
SL2(Z) and some character of order dividing 12.
For M = 1, i.e. for the symplectic fermion vertex operator superalgebra (which
is actually lisse and not just quasi-lisse.), the modular transformation properties
are explained, for example, in [FGR22].
We shall see in Section 7.4 that the expression for the character chV (∆)(q) of the
boundary vertex operator superalgebra is quite a bit more complicated than the
supercharacter (and in particular depends not only on M), but still satisfies good
modularity properties, at least in those examples where we are able to bring the
character into a nice form.

(see, e.g., [BFR12, FOR13]).

In general, we expect the character of Vmin(∆) to be some kind of partial or false
theta function of rank M, times something modular.
(Higher-rank) partial or false theta functions themselves are not modular in
the usual sense. However, they are closely related to mock theta functions and
quantum modular forms (see, e.g., [BFR12, FOR13]). As we are mainly interested
in the boundary hypertoric vertex operator algebras in this paper, we forego a more
detailed investigation of the false theta functions appearing in this work.
7.4. Boundary Character. Comparing the minimal character in Section 7.3 to
the boundary supercharacter in Section 7.2, we saw that the partial theta functions
were summed to η(q)2M, which is modular of weight M (though it may be more
useful to interpret it as participating in a vector-valued modular form of weight 0
with log(q)-terms in other components). While the boundary supercharacters take
a very simple form, we expect the boundary characters to be more interesting,
while still being (quasi)modular. In the following, we determine these characters
as modular forms in some classes of examples and make a conjecture about the
general behaviour.

7.5. Comparison of Minimal and Boundary Characters. We saw in Sec-
tion 7.3 and Section 7.4 that the (super)characters of the minimal and boundary
hypertoric vertex (operator)superalgebras Vmin(∆) and V (∆) have quite different
modular behaviour. This is expected, as the latter are quasi-lisse and the former
in general are not.
In the following, we explain how the partial theta series appearing in the minimal
characters chVmin(∆)(q) are paired with usual theta series (which are modular) and
then summed up to a quasimodular form.
To this end, we rewrite the sum expression for the (super)character from Re-
mark 7.4 and Proposition 7.8 as

The above character decomposition is simply a consequence of Remark 3.25,
where we describe V (∆) as a ZM-simple-current extension of the intermediate ver-

7.6. Characters and Free-Field Realisations. In this section, so far, we have
computed the characters of the minimal and boundary hypertoric vertex operator
(super)algebras Vmin(∆) and V (∆) by applying the Euler-Poincaré principle to the
defining BRST reduction, i.e. to the upper row in the diagrams in Proposition 3.9
and Proposition 3.10.
On the other hand, one can also study the characters by looking at the right
column in these diagrams, i.e. based on the free-field realisation described by the
screening kernels. This amounts to writing down Felder-style complexes and com-
puting the characters by applying the Euler-Poincaré principle to those.
We demonstrate here how this works in the case of the (super)character of the
smallest example discussed in Section 3.3.2, i.e. for N = M = 1 and ∆= (1), which
already manifests the essential features. Recall that in this case V (∆) = SF is the
symplectic fermion vertex operator superalgebra, whereas Vmin(∆) = M(2) is the
p = 2 singlet vertex operator algebra. Thus,
schV (∆)(q) = η(q)2 = q1/12(q; q)2

Appendix A. Quiver Varieties
In this section, we discuss a special case of hypertoric varieties, namely those
coming from quivers (see, e.g., [HS02] and [Nag21]). They are called hypertoric
quiver varieties and are precisely the Nakajima quiver varieties coming from quivers
with 1-dimensional gauge nodes [Nak94, Nak98].
Let Q = (V, E) be a finite, directed graph with |V | = M + 1 nodes
V = {n0, n1, . . . , nM}
and |E| = N edges
E = {ek
ij := (i, j) | k = 1, . . . , mij},
where mij ∈Z>0 is the multiplicity of the edge (i, j). We assume that Q is (weakly)
connected and has no self-loops. In particular, N ≥M. The choice of orientation
of Q will not affect the resulting hypertoric variety up to isomorphism of Poisson
varieties (see Remark 2.22 in [Nag21]).
Then, consider the free Z-modules ZM+1 with basis the nodes {n0, . . . , nM} and
ZN with basis the edges {ek
ij}. We consider the rank-M submodule ZM ⊂ZM+1
with basis {n0 −n1, n0 −n2, . . . , n0 −nM}, i.e. the Z-linear combinations of nodes
whose coefficients sum to zero. The (M × N)-matrix ∆is now defined by the map

Given an effective stability parameter δ ∈QM, we denote by
Yδ(Q) := Yδ(∆)
the (projective) hypertoric quiver variety associated with the quiver Q.
Recall
that ∆is always unimodular in the quiver case. Hence, if δ is generic, Yδ(Q) is
smooth, of dimension 2(N −M) = 2(|E| −|V | + 1) and a symplectic resolution of
Y0(Q) := Y0(∆).
Two examples of quiver hypertoric varieties are discussed in Section 2.11 and
Section 2.12; see Figure 4 and Figure 5, respectively.
We can bring the above hypertoric quiver varieties into the form of (framed)
Nakajima quiver varieties. To this end, we consider the quiver Q and “expand” it
at n0. Without loss of generality, we assume that the node n0 is a source, i.e. has
only outgoing edges. Then, we remove the node n0 and the nodes V = {n1, . . . , nM}

The cotangent bundle T ∗R(Q, v, w) is canonically isomorphic to the representa-
tion space of the doubled quiver T ∗R(Q, v, w) ∼= R(Q#, v, w), where the definition
of the latter implicitly assumes that also the framing edges are doubled. This is the
reason why, up to isomorphism of Poisson varieties, the quiver variety only depends
on Q# and not on Q.
The additional coordinates for the cotangent space T ∗R(Q, v, w) ∼= T ∗V are y(k)
